How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

The data analyzed here were collected as part of the Mainz Network Study and have been disseminated separately as a data publication (Hülsemann et al., 2026), which describes the sample and measures but does not report the analyses or conclusions presented in this manuscript. We have no conflicts of interest to disclose.

[div-01]

Why faster thinkers are smarter thinkers: Working memory binding as the link between processing speed and cognitive ability One of the oldest observations in psychology is that faster thinkers tend to be smarter thinkersbut after more than a century of research, we still cannot adequately explain why. Since Galton's (1883) proposal that the speed of mental operations may reflect intellectual power, the relationship between processing speed and cognitive ability has become one of the most robust findings in individual differences research. Meta-analyses consistently demonstrate that individuals who score higher on cognitive ability tests process information more quickly across a broad range of tasks, with latent correlations between processing speed factors and general cognitive ability (g) reaching magnitudes of .50 to .80 (Doebler & Scheffler, 2016; Lerche et al., 2020; Schubert, 2019; Sheppard & Vernon, 2008). This association holds regardless of whether processing speed is indexed at the neural level through event-related potential (ERP) latencies, or at the cognitive level through diffusion model drift rates that capture the efficiency of evidence accumulation, as long as speed is measured during goal-directed, decision-related information processing (Sadus et al., 2026; Schubert & Frischkorn, 2020).

What makes this association theoretically puzzling is the vast gap in complexity between the tasks that reveal it. Elementary cognitive tasksdeciding whether an arrow points left or right or judging whether two letters have the same nameare trivially easy, with accuracy rates near ceiling. Cognitive ability tests, by contrast, require complex relational reasoning, pattern induction, and the integration of multiple pieces of information.

The content overlap between these two classes of tasks is essentially zero. The strong relationship between them therefore cannot be explained through shared task content but demands a mechanistic account: some cognitive process must be shared across both domains, one that covaries with processing speed and is also associated with performance on complex reasoning tasks.

A prominent theoretical account proposes that this mediating process resides in working memory: faster processing is hypothesized to enable more efficient maintenance and manipulation of information, which in turn supports the complex reasoning that defines g (Fry & Hale, 1996; Salthouse, 1996). The most direct empirical evidence for this idea stems from developmental research, showing that developmental increases in processing speed precede increases in working memory capacity, which in turn precede increases in fluid cognitive ability (Fry & Hale, 1996 , 2000; Kail, 2007; Kail et al., 2016; Nettelbeck & Burns, 2010; Tourva & Spanoudis, 2020). In adults, the ingredients for this account are well-established: latent variable studies consistently show that both processing speed and working memory capacity predict cognitive ability as highly correlated predictors with largely overlapping variance (Frischkorn et al., 2019; Löffler et al., 2024; Schmiedek et al., 2007). Together, these findings are consistent with a mediation account in which working memory capacity may serve as a statistical intermediary between processing speed and cognitive ability. However, formal tests of this indirect pathway, estimating whether the speed-g association is reduced or eliminated when working memory is included as an intermediary, are lacking in adults.

Moreover, theories of working memory distinguish multiple component processes, and the question of which specific process would carry the link between processing speed and cognitive ability remains contested.

The influential executive-attention account proposes that both working memory capacity and reasoning ability rely on a domain-general capacity for attention controlthe ability to maintain goal-relevant information in the face of interference and distraction (Engle, 2002 (Engle, , 2018;; Kane et al., 2007). On this account, the association between processing speed and cognitive ability would be carried by working memory capacity to the extent that working memory tasks capture executive attention demands. Processing speed is assumed to either support attention control by enabling faster refreshing of goal-relevant representations or to directly reflect it, as Mashburn et al. (2024) have argued that individual differences in drift rates on elementary cognitive tasks index the efficiency of attentional engagement with taskrelevant information rather than a domain-general speed parameter. Complex span tasks, in which individuals must maintain memoranda while performing a distracting secondary task, are specifically designed to tax these executive demands and serve as the canonical measure of executive working memory.

An alternative account locates the critical process not in executive attention but in the ability to form and maintain temporary bindings in memory (Oberauer, 2019; Oberauer et al., 2007 Oberauer et al., , 2008;; Wilhelm et al., 2013). Consider a juggler keeping multiple balls in the air. What limits performance is not hand speed alone, but the fact that speed determines how many balls can be caught and re-launched before any single one fallsthat is, how many elements can be maintained in a coordinated pattern simultaneously. Analogous logic applies to the relationship between processing speed and working memory. According to the binding account, the core function of working memory is to form and maintain temporary associations between elements that do not share a pre-existing relationship: a letter with a spatial location, a word with a number, or a shape with a color (Oberauer, 2009). These bindings are inherently fragile; they are vulnerable to interference from subsequent input. According to this account, faster cognitive processing would facilitate binding by enabling the more rapid formation and refreshing of these associations before they degrade, thereby allowing a greater number of intact bindings to be maintained simultaneously. Moreover, faster neural processingas indexed by latencies of the P3 component associated with the rapid transmission of information from frontal attention and working memory processes to temporal-parietal processes of memory storage (Polich, 2007) might directly facilitate the building and maintenance of arbitrary bindings by enabling efficient transfer between frontal and parietal regions (Schubert et al., 2017).

Binding is theoretically central to complex cognition because reasoning tasks require the simultaneous maintenance of multiple relational structures (Chuderski, 2014; Halford et al., 1998; Schubert, Löffler, Sadus, et al., 2023), though recent evidence suggests that its role is more elementary than the demands of reasoning tasks might imply (Chuderski, 2019; Jastrzębski et al., 2020). Solving a matrix reasoning problem, for example, requires identifying and maintaining feature-to-position and rule-to-dimension bindings, and it would be natural to suppose that what matters is the number or complexity of the structures so maintained. Chuderski (2019), however, found that processing a single trivial binding between two symbols predicts fluid intelligence, and Jastrzębski et al. (2020) found that a factor defined by tasks requiring the validation of a single predefined relation was statistically equivalent to a fluid reasoning factor defined by hallmark reasoning tests, sharing most of the residual variance not attributable to working memory. On this evidence, the operations most closely associated with reasoning (i.e., rule discovery, rule integration, the parallel maintenance of multiple structures) are not what makes relational processing consequential for cognitive ability. What appears to matter is the fidelity of the elementary binding operation itself. This has a direct implication for the present study: if it holds, structurally simple binding tasks should be at least as diagnostic of the processes underlying cognitive ability as tasks that require additional dual-task coordination or the maintenance of memoranda against concurrent distraction during the retention interval. Importantly, working memory binding tasks are not free of interference, as proactive interference from bindings encoded on previous trials competes with current-trial bindings at retrieval (Oberauer & Lin, 2017), but this interference arises from the memory content itself rather than from an imposed secondary task, and managing it does not require the executive coordination demands that define complex span performance.

The question of which component of working memory carries its association with cognitive ability predates the present study by two decades. Unsworth and Engle (2007) argued that simple and complex span tasks differ mostly in the degree to which they require maintenance in primary memory and retrieval from secondary memory, and that both tasks therefore relate to higher-order ability through partly distinct routes. Colom et al. (2008) modeled short-term storage, updating, attention control, and mental speed concurrently with working memory capacity and intelligence, concluding that short-term storage accounted for most of the working memory-intelligence relationship, with attention control contributing little unique variance. Martínez et al. (2011) reached a similar conclusion, and Shipstead et al. (2014) proposed a decomposition into primary memory, secondary memory, and attention control in which the components contribute differentially across criterion abilities. Yet another suggestion was put forward by Oberauer et al. (2008), arguing that the ability to build and maintain arbitrary bindings underlies the associations of both simple and complex span with cognitive ability. The debate remains unresolved (Colom et al., 2016). Our contrast between binding and complex span can be seen as a continuation of this debate. What distinguishes the present study is not the contrast itself but its embedding in the speedworking memory-g triangle: prior work has compared working memory components as predictors of cognitive ability, whereas the question here is which component reproduces the association between processing speed and cognitive ability.

Although both the executive attention and the binding account predict that working memory mediates the speed-cognition association, they make different predictions about which component of working memory carries the link. Distinguishing between them requires comparing working memory tasks that differ in their demands: complex span tasks, which impose substantial executive attention demands, versus binding tasks, which isolate the ability to form and maintain temporary associations without dual-task coordination or concurrent distraction management. This comparison has not been made, for two reasons. First, no study has formally tested whether working memory capacity mediates the speed-g association in adults using latent variable mediation models that control for measurement error. Second, and more importantly, complex span tasks are process-impure with respect to the two accounts under investigation: they require not only executive attention (maintaining memoranda against distraction) but also binding (associating memoranda with serial positions and maintaining those associations across the retention interval). This means that even if complex span tasks mediate the speedcognition association, such a result would be ambiguous: it could reflect executive attention, binding, or both. What is needed is a direct comparison between complex span tasks and simpler binding tasks that do not involve dual-task coordination or interference management (see Figure 1). If binding tasks, which isolate relational maintenance with minimal executive demands, account for the speed-cognition association as strongly as or more strongly than complex span tasks, this would favor the binding account. If complex span tasks account for the association above and beyond binding, this would favor the executive attention account.

Figure 1 Competing Theoretical Accounts of the Association Between Processing Speed and General Cognitive Ability

Note. Panel A depicts the executive attention account, in which complex span tasks, indexing executive attention through dual-task coordination and interference management, serve as the intermediary between processing speed and general cognitive ability. Panel B depicts the binding account, in which simpler binding tasks, indexing relational maintenance without executive demands, serve as the intermediary. Panel C shows the competitive model that adjudicates between the two accounts. The critical feature is the task impurity of complex span: because complex span tasks impose both executive attention and binding demands, they share variance with binding tasks. If the binding path retains a unique association with general cognitive ability while the complex span path does not, the speed-cognition association is carried by relational binding rather than executive attention. Dashed lines represent the direct path that is tested against zero in each model. This explanatory demand does not depend on processing speed being the strongest correlate of general cognitive ability. Under a mediation account, however, that is the expected pattern rather than a difficulty for it. If the speed-g association is carried entirely by an intermediate construct, the speed-g correlation equals the product of the two correlations flanking that construct and is therefore necessarily the smaller of them. The question is accordingly not which construct is most strongly associated with g, but how the covariance among the three is structured and which working memory process reproduces the speed-g association once it is included in the model.

A prior question concerns what a cross-sectional design of this kind can establish. The developmental cascade literature motivates an ordering in which processing speed precedes working memory, which in turn precedes fluid ability, but that ordering is an assumption we import rather than a result we can produce. Three latent variables connected in a chain admit two orderings that imply identical covariance structures that are statistically indistinguishable.

What such data can identify is the position a construct occupies: a chain in which B is intermediate implies that A and C are conditionally independent given B, and this constraint differs from the constraints implied by placing A or C in the middle. The question we address is accordingly not whether processing speed is causally prior to working memory, but which working memory process occupies the intermediate position between processing speed and general cognitive ability and whether that position is better occupied by the executive attention demands captured by complex span tasks or by the relational binding shared across working memory paradigms.

The present study

The present study provides a direct test of whether the association between processing speed and complex cognition is better accounted for by executive attention or by temporary binding processes. We operationalized processing speed using two complementary approaches: ERP component peak latencies, which index the timing of neurocognitive processing, and diffusion-model drift rates, which estimate the speed of evidence accumulation separately from response caution and nondecision time. This combination of neurophysiological and model-based behavioral measures allowed us to assess whether the findings converged across measurement approaches. In both cases, processing speed was treated as a latent construct inferred from multiple indicators rather than as a directly observed process. We then tested three competing structural models using latent variable modeling. In the executive-attention model, complex span performance serves as the intermediary between processing speed and g, testing whether the speed-cognition association is accounted for by working memory capacity as indexed by tasks with high executive demands (see Figure 1A).

In the binding model, binding task performance serves as the intermediary, testing whether simpler tasks that isolate relational maintenance without dual-task coordination can account for the same association (see Figure 1B). In the competitive model, complex span and binding performance are entered as parallel intermediaries, providing the critical adjudication (see Figure 1C): this model tests which component process accounts for the speed-cognition association when both are modeled simultaneously, and whether either accounts for the association above and beyond the other. These three models yield distinct predictions. If the executive attention account is correct, complex span performance should account for the speed-cognition association to a greater extent than binding tasks and should retain a significant unique indirect effect in the competitive model. If the binding account is correct, binding tasks should account for the association at least as strongly as complex span tasks despite their simpler task demands and should retain a significant unique indirect effect in the competitive model even when complex span is included. If both processes contribute independently, both should show significant unique indirect effects when modeled in parallel. The multimethod design with converging neural and cognitive operationalizations of processing speed provides an additional test of robustness, as a finding that holds across both measurement levels is unlikely to reflect method-specific artifacts.

Adjudicating between these accounts has implications beyond the speed-cognition association. If binding rather than complex span statistically accounted for the speed-g association, this would challenge the prevailing view that working memory's relation to cognition primarily reflects its executive, attention-demanding aspects and would instead favor an account centered on the formation and maintenance of relational representations.

Method Sample

Participants were recruited from the community through newspaper articles, radio broadcasts, a podcast, flyers, and a university participant database as part of the Mainz Network Study, a larger project on individual differences in cognition (Hülsemann et al., 2026). Eligible individuals were aged 18-40, reported no MRI contraindications (e.g., claustrophobia, pregnancy, severe overweight, extreme back pain, cardiac pacemaker, ferromagnetic implants; retainers permitted), and no conditions likely to impede EEG recording (e.g., skin allergies). Further exclusion criteria were current psychiatric or neurological treatment, self-reported mental disorders or any condition that affects normal functioning of the central nervous system, use of substances affecting the central nervous system (e.g., psychotropic medication), and color-vision deficits.

The final sample consisted of N = 215 with a mean age of M = 27.71 years (SD = 6.76, range: 18-40). When asked about their sex, 113 participants identified as women and 102 identified as men. We collected no information about participants' ethnicity. The participants' highest educational qualifications were distributed as follows: 39.1% held a high school diploma as their highest qualification, 30.2% a university degree, 14.0% a technical college degree, 6.0% a vocational diploma, 5.1% a doctorate, and 5.6% a lower secondary qualification or no formal diploma. Regarding occupation, 51.6% were employed, 34.0% were university students, 4.7% were secondary school students, 4.2% were self-employed, and the remainder were job seekers, volunteers, or homemakers.

The sample size yieldsassuming a Type I error-rate of α = .05 -a power of 1 -β = .65 to test the hypothesis of close fit (H0: ε ≤ 0.05, H1: ε ≥ 0.08) for the structural equation model (SEM) with the fewest degrees of freedom (df = 33; see below) reported in the present study, and a power of 1 -β > .80 for all other models (Browne & Cudeck, 1992).

Materials

All tasks were administered as part of the Mainz Network Study (Hülsemann et al., 2026); detailed task descriptions, stimuli specifications, and data are available in the associated data publication. Tasks were presented in a fixed order within each session to minimize between-participant error variance (Goodhew & Edwards, 2019).

Cognitive Ability Tests

General cognitive ability was assessed with two measures. The short version of the Berlin Intelligence Structure Test (BIS; Jäger et al., 1997) is a paper-and-pencil test comprising 15 tasks that fully crosses four operation-related components (processing capacity, processing speed, memory, and creativity) with three content-related components (verbal, numerical, figural). Composite scores for each operation-related component were computed by aggregating normalized z-scores across tasks within the respective component.

In addition, participants completed a computerized, untimed version of Raven's Advanced Progressive Matrices (APM; Raven et al., 1994), in which they selected the missing element from a 3 × 3 matrix pattern across 36 items. One participant's APM data were excluded for performing at or below chance level, as determined by a one-sided exact binomial test (α = .05; minimum correct for above-chance performance: 9 of 36). For the remaining participants, items were assigned to three parcels in a round-robin fashion (items 1, 4, 7, … sorted into Parcel 1; items 2, 5, 8, … sorted into Parcel 2; items 3, 6, 9, … sorted into Parcel 3), and parcel scores were computed as percentage correct.

Working Memory Tasks

Working memory capacity was assessed with two types of tasks representing the two theoretical accounts under investigation. Complex span tasks. Three complex span tasks with individually calibrated response deadlines for the secondary processing speed task (Unsworth et al., 2005) were administered using the German adaptations by Rummel et al. (2019). In the operation span task (15 trials), participants alternated between verifying arithmetic equations and encoding letters (3-7 per trial, 800 ms each) for subsequent serial recall. The reading span task (15 trials) was structurally identical but required semantic plausibility judgments of sentences as the processing component. In the symmetry span task (12 trials, 800 ms each), participants alternated between symmetry judgments of geometric figures and encoding highlighted positions in a 4 × 4 grid (2-5 per trial, 800 ms each) for subsequent serial recall. Performance was scored as the number of items recalled in the correct serial position.

Binding tasks.

Three binding tasks were administered following Wilhelm et al. (2013). In the letter-color task (15 trials), participants studied sequentially presented lettercolor pairs (2-6 per trial, 1000 ms each) and subsequently recalled the binding partner of a probed element from among all elements used in that trial. The word-number task (14 trials) followed the same structure with noun-number pairs presented for 2000 ms each. In the location-letter task (14 trials), participants studied letters appearing sequentially in cells of a 3 × 3 grid (1500 ms each) and subsequently recalled either the letter belonging to a probed location or the location belonging to a probed letter. Across all binding tasks, 50% of probes tested each element type (e.g., letter or color). Performance was scored as proportion correct within each task.

Processing Speed Tasks

Processing speed was assessed with three elementary cognitive tasks (ECTs) during concurrent EEG recording. In the Posner letter matching task (PLM; Posner & Mitchell, 1967; 120 trials), participants judged whether two letters were semantically identical regardless of case. In the Sternberg memory scanning task (SMS; Sternberg, 1969; 100 trials), participants memorized a sequence of five digits and subsequently judged whether a probe digit had been part of the sequence. In the two-choice reaction time task (CRT; Schubert et al., 2015; 100 trials), participants indicated the side on which a target appeared in one of two flanking squares. Across all three tasks, target stimuli were presented until response with a maximum of 3000 ms. Participants were instructed to respond as quickly and accurately as possible.

Cognitive processing speed.

Reaction time data were preprocessed in three steps: (1) trials with no responses within the 3 s time limit were removed, (2) anticipatory responses with reaction times below 150 ms were removed, and (3) the remaining reaction times were log-transformed and z-standardized within each participant, and trials with |z| > 3 were excluded. Across tasks, this procedure removed 0.95% (PLM), 1.46% (SMS), and 1.36% (CRT) of trials.

We then estimated drift diffusion model (DDM) parameters from the preprocessed single-trial data using fast-dm (Voss & Voss, 2007) with maximum likelihood estimation.

Drift rate (v), boundary separation (a), non-decision time (t0), and non-decision time variability (st0) were freely estimated; starting point was fixed at z = 0.5 (i.e., no a priori response bias). Participant-task combinations were flagged if parameter estimates fell outside plausible ranges (e.g., a < 0.5 or > 5.0, v < 0 or > 20, t0 < 0.10 s or > 0.80 s, st0 = 0 or st0 > t0).

Estimates from participants with more than one flag per task were set to missing (CRT: n = 17; SMS: n = 3; PLM: n = 0). Drift rates served as indicators of cognitive processing speed.

Neural processing speed.

Peak latencies of the P2, N2, and P3 components, extracted from the CRT, SMS, and PLM tasks (see below), served as indicators of neural processing speed.

Procedure

The study comprised three sessions conducted in fixed order for all but seven participants, who completed sessions in a different order due to scheduling constraints.

Participants provided written informed consent at the beginning of the first session and were screened for color vision deficits using the Ishihara Tests for Colour-Blindness (Ishihara, 1917). They received €100 for completing all sessions and, upon request, individualized feedback on their cognitive test performance. The study was approved by the local ethics committee of Johannes Gutenberg University Mainz (reference: 2021-JGU-psychEK-012).

Data were collected between February 2024 and February 2025.

In the first session that took about four hours, groups of one to seven participants completed the BIS as a paper-and-pencil test administered by a trained experimenter, followed by the binding tasks, the complex span tasks, and the APM on individual computers at their own pace. In the second session that took about three hours, participants completed different tasks individually in a dimly lit, sound-attenuated, electrically shielded recording cabin while EEG was recorded. They completed a cued task-switching task (data not reported here), the Posner letter-matching task, the Sternberg memory scanning task, and the twochoice reaction time task in fixed order, with a two-minute resting-state EEG recorded before each task (data not reported here). Of the 215 participants who completed the first session, 212 completed the second session (3 dropouts). Inter-session intervals were not fixed but scheduled according to participant and study capacity; the median interval between the first and second session was 19 days (M = 26, SD = 26, range: 1-242). A third session involving MRI acquisition was also conducted as part of the larger project but is not relevant to the present analyses.

EEG Recording

EEG was recorded continuously from 60 equidistant Ag/AgCl active electrodes (EasyCap GmbH, Woerthsee, Germany) using an actiCHamp Plus amplifier (Brain Products GmbH, Gilching, Germany). An additional electrode was placed below the left eye to record the electrooculogram (EOG). All channels were referenced online to electrode Cz and recorded in DC mode at a sampling rate of 1000 Hz with 0.05 µV resolution. A fronto-lateral electrode close to F2 in the 10-20 system was used as ground. Electrode impedances were kept below 20 kΩ for all but nine participants (for further details, see Hülsemann et al., 2026).

Participants were seated in a dimly lit, sound-attenuated, electrically shielded recording cabin and instructed to minimize movement during task performance.

EEG Data Processing

EEG data were preprocessed using the EEGLAB toolbox (version 2025.1.0; Delorme & Makeig, 2004) in MATLAB (version R2023b; The MathWorks, Inc.). Data were segmented from the beginning to the end of each task with a 1-minute buffer on each side. Line noise at 50 and 100 Hz was removed using CleanLine (Mullen, 2012) on a temporarily 1-Hz high-pass filtered copy of the data (Widmann et al., 2015), following the PREP pipeline approach (Bigdely-Shamlo et al., 2015); the correction was then transferred back to the unfiltered signal by subtracting the filtered copy and adding back the cleaned version. Noisy channels were identified on the filtered copy using the clean_artifacts function (Kothe, 2019) and removed from the reconstructed signal if they correlated less than .80 with neighboring channels. The online reference (Cz) was included as a zero channel, the data were re-referenced to the average, and Cz was subsequently removed to maintain full rank for independent component analysis (ICA).

For ICA, a separate processing branch was created: the data were high-pass filtered at 1 Hz (zero-phase Hamming-windowed sinc FIR filter; Widmann et al., 2015), segmented into consecutive 2-s epochs, and artefactual epochs were rejected using absolute amplitude (±1000 µV), probability, and kurtosis criteria (5 SD for single channels, 2 SD for all channels; Delorme et al., 2007). ICA was performed using the extended infomax algorithm (Bell & Sejnowski, 1995; Lee et al., 1999) with a reduced learning rate (1 × 10⁻⁵, compared to the default of approximately 0.00065/log(nchannels) in EEGLAB's runica) and an increased maximum number of training steps (2,000, compared to the default of 512) to improve the stability of the decomposition. Components were classified using ICLabel (Pion-Tonachini et al., 2019) and removed if their highest probability corresponded to eye (≥ 25%), muscle (≥ 90%), or heart (≥ 60%) activity. The resulting ICA weight matrix was applied to the continuous, unfiltered data. Removed channels and the online reference were restored via spherical spline interpolation.

Subsequently, the preprocessed continuous EEG data were band-pass filtered with a low cutoff of 0.1 Hz and a high cutoff of 16 Hz. Epochs were extracted in a window of 1 s after stimulus onset with a baseline window of 0.2 s preceding stimulus onset. Artefactual epochs were rejected using the same criteria as above but with a stricter amplitude threshold of ±500 µV. Incorrect trials and trials with reaction times below 0.15 s or above 3 s or with logtransformed reaction times exceeding ±3 SD of the participant mean were discarded.

Consistent with previous work from our lab (Schubert et al., 2017; Schubert, Löffler, Hagemann, et al., 2023), we extracted P2 peak latencies after visual inspection of each participant's ERP at a frontal electrode over midline (close to FCz in the 10-20 system), and N2 and P3 peak latencies at a parietal electrode over midline (close to Pz in the 10-20 system) from each task for use as indicators of neural processing speed.

Data Analysis

Anthropic Claude Opus (versions 4.6, 5.0, and 5.5; Anthropic, 2026) was used as a coding assistant during data analysis and visualization. Specifically, the tool was used to assist with writing and debugging R code for data preprocessing and analysis, to refine R code for the figures reported in the manuscript, and to refine the documentation of analysis scripts. All analytic decisions, model specifications, and theoretical interpretations are the authors' own. AI-assisted code was reviewed and validated by the authors before inclusion in the analysis pipeline.

Outlier Exclusion

One participant was excluded from the APM for performing at or below chance level (see above). One additional participant was excluded from all complex span tasks based on visual inspection of response patterns. For one participant, reading span data were unavailable due to a technical error during administration. Across all remaining measures, participantlevel values exceeding ±3 SD from the sample mean were set to missing on a variable-byvariable basis. For behavioral measures from the first session, this criterion was the primary source of missingness, removing between 0 and 5 observations per indicator (0-2.3%) and yielding between 209 and 215 valid observations per behavioral variable. Of the 215 participants who completed the first session, 212 completed the EEG session (3 dropouts); additional missingness in processing speed measures arose from ERP component identification issues (yielding 199-212 valid ERP latency estimates per component and task) and from DDM estimation failures for participants with implausible parameter estimates (CRT: n = 17; SMS: n = 3; PLM: n = 0). After applying the ±3 SD criterion to the processing speed indicators, between 193 and 210 valid observations remained per indicator. All variables were z-standardized, and missing data were accommodated using full information maximum likelihood estimation.

Statistical Analyses

We used structural equation modeling to test whether working memory capacity mediated the relationship between processing speed and general cognitive ability. Processing speed was operationalized separately as cognitive processing speed (drift rates) and neural processing speed (ERP latencies). These two operationalizations were modeled in separate SEMs throughout all analysesthat is, each mediation model was estimated twice, once with neural processing speed and once with cognitive processing speed as the predictor. We first tested whether the speed-g relationship was accounted for by complex span performance, then whether it was accounted for by binding task performance, and finally whether complex span or binding performance mediated the relationship above and beyond the other. Mediation was evaluated by comparing nested models in which the direct path from speed to g was freely estimated versus constrained to zero; indirect effects were tested using the delta method and additionally evaluated using bias-corrected bootstrap confidence intervals (5,000 resamples).

Throughout the analyses reported below, we use the term mediation in its statistical sense: a reduction or elimination of the association between an independent variable and a dependent variable when a third variable is included in the model. We do not intend this terminology to imply that the present data establish causal direction. The hypothesized ordering is motivated by developmental evidence showing that age-related increases in processing speed temporally precede gains in working memory capacity, which in turn precede gains in fluid ability and by the theoretical consideration that processing speed is a more elementary computational property than the capacity to maintain relational bindings (Fry & Hale, 1996 , 2000). However, cross-sectional mediation cannot rule out alternative causal architectures, including common-cause models. The structural models tested here should therefore be understood as evaluating the statistical consistency of competing theoretical accounts with the observed covariance structure rather than as establishing causal pathways. We return to this issue in the Discussion.

General cognitive ability was specified using a bifactor model in which g loaded directly on all seven indicators (the four BIS operation-related component scores and the three APM item parcels) with an orthogonal APM-specific factor capturing method variance shared among the APM parcels but unrelated to g. The APM-specific factor was constrained to be uncorrelated with all other latent variables to ensure that the structural paths targeted g proper.

Neural processing speed was modeled as a higher-order factor with three first-order component factors (P2, N2, P3), each indicated by peak latencies from the three elementary cognitive tasks; the N2 residual variance was constrained to zero to resolve a boundary estimate. Cognitive processing speed was modeled as a single latent factor indicated by the three drift rate estimates. Complex span was modeled as a single factor indicated by operation span, reading span, and symmetry span scores. Binding was modeled as a single factor indicated by letter-color, word-number, and location-letter task scores.

Models were fitted using the R package lavaan (Rosseel, 2012) with maximum likelihood estimation. Missing data were accommodated using full information maximum likelihood. We evaluated model fit using the comparative fit index (CFI; Bentler, 1990) and the root mean square error of approximation (RMSEA; Browne & Cudeck, 1992), with values of CFI ≥ .95 and RMSEA ≤ .06 indicating good fit and CFI ≥ .90 and RMSEA ≤ .08 indicating acceptable fit as conventional guidelines (Browne & Cudeck, 1992; Hu & Bentler, 1999). However, these conventional thresholds were derived from simulations of simple models with few factors and indicators and do not generalize straightforwardly to the complex measurement structures employed here. Simulation work has demonstrated that the CFI systematically decreases as the number of observed variables and latent factors increases, even when the model is correctly specified in the population (Kenny & McCoach, 2003; Shi et al., 2019). In models with a large number of indicators and multiple latent factors, CFI values in the range of .85-.90 can correspond to the same degree of approximate fit that produces CFI > .95 in simpler models. The RMSEA, which adjusts for model complexity through its degrees-of-freedom correction, is less susceptible to this bias and may therefore provide a more appropriate index of fit for the present models (Kenny & McCoach, 2003).

We consequently interpret model fit primarily through the RMSEA and its confidence interval, using CFI as a supplementary index while recognizing its downward bias in complex measurement structures like those in the present study. Nested models were compared using likelihood ratio tests and Akaike weights (Wagenmakers & Farrell, 2004). Statistical significance of individual parameters was evaluated at α = .05 using the Wald z-test. Path coefficients were tested on the unstandardized scale; standardized coefficients (β) and standardized 95% confidence intervals (95% CIs) are reported as effect-size indices.foot_0 Bayesian robustness analyses. Because the latent complex span and binding factors were highly correlated, we additionally fit Bayesian versions of these models to enable more stable probabilistic comparisons of competing indirect effects. Bayesian SEMs were estimated in blavaan (Merkle et al., 2021) using weakly informative priors; inference is summarized using 95% credible intervals and posterior probabilities. Full details on priors, Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) settings, and convergence diagnostics are provided in the Supplementary Materials (Bayesian Robustness Analysis).

Transparency and Openness

We report on how we determined our sample size, all data exclusions, and all measures in the study. The raw data, preprocessed data, and code supporting the main findings of the study, including R and software versions for fully reproducible analyses, are available in the Open Science Framework repository at https://osf.io/fm9qh (Schubert et al., 2026). Further access to the data and materials (except for the licensed cognitive ability tests) are available through the larger study's dataset publication (Hülsemann et al., 2026). Neither the study nor the analyses were preregistered.

Results

Descriptive statistics for all variables are shown in Table 1, zero-order correlations between all variables included in SEMs are shown in Figure 2 and Supplementary Table S1, and latent correlations among all constructs are shown in Table 2. Although complex span tasks correlated somewhat more strongly than binding tasks with most indicators of cognitive ability (Figure 2), the latent correlation with g was higher for binding than complex span (Table 2). The binding tasks shared much less variance with one another than the complex span tasks did, yet correlated with the intelligence indicators nearly as strongly as with each other. Therefore, the variance they did share was almost entirely related to g.

Processing Speed and General Cognitive Ability

Processing speed was substantially associated with general cognitive ability at both levels of analysis. 2 In a model regressing g directly on neural processing speed (ERP peak latencies; χ²(100) = 174.59, p < .001, CFI = 0.92, RMSEA = 0.06, 90% CI [0.04, 0.07]), faster neural processing was associated with higher cognitive ability (β = -0.48, 95% CI [-0.63, -0.34], p < .001), with processing speed accounting for 24% of the variance in g. An analogous model with cognitive processing speed (drift rates) as the predictor fit the data well, χ²(33) = 68.97, p < .001, CFI = 0.95, RMSEA = 0.07, 90% CI [0.05, 0.09], and yielded a comparable association, β = 0.48, 95% CI [0.32, 0.63], p < .001, R² = .23. Note that the sign 2 Because the Berlin Intelligence Structure Test includes a processing speed component (BIS-PS) and a memory component (BIS-M) as indicators of g, one might worry that processing speed and working memory capacity predict g in part because g is in part defined by clerical processing speed and memory tasks. To address this concern, we refit all models excluding both BIS-PS and BIS-M subscales. All substantive conclusions were unchanged. In particular, binding continued to fully mediate the cognitive processing speed-g relationship and to emerge as the dominant mediator in the parallel models, while complex span continued to show only partial mediation (though both pathways fell short of significance due to the same collinearity constraints observed in the main analyses). The direct paths from processing speed to g remained significant in the complex span models and nonsignificant in the binding models, replicating the pattern from the main analyses.

reversal relative to the ERP model reflects the directionality of the two speed measures: shorter ERP latencies indicate faster neural processing, whereas higher drift rates indicate faster evidence accumulation, both indexing greater processing efficiency. These total effects establish the baseline relationship that subsequent mediation analyses seek to explain: approximately one quarter of the individual differences in general cognitive ability can be attributed to differences in the speed with which people process elementary cognitive operations, whether measured at the neural or behavioral level. The central question is whether this association is statistically accounted for by working memory capacity, and if so, through which componentthe executive attention demands captured by complex span tasks, or the relational binding processes shared across working memory paradigms.

Does Executive Working Memory Account for the Processing Speed-g Relationship?

We first tested whether complex span performance mediated the speed-g relationship. 3 Model fit was acceptable to good for both the neural processing speed model, χ²(146) = 249.84, p < .001, CFI = 0.92, RMSEA = 0.06, 90% CI [0.05, 0.07], and the cognitive processing speed model, χ²(61) = 116.81, p < .001, CFI = 0.94, RMSEA = 0.07, 90% CI [0.05, 0.08]. Standardized path estimates for both models are shown in Figure 3.

In both models, processing speed significantly predicted complex span performance (βneural = -0.41, 95% CI [-0.56, -0.25]; βcognitive = 0.44, 95% CI [0.28, 0.59]; ps < .001), and complex span performance significantly predicted g (βneural = 0.52, 95% CI [0.37, 0.66]; βcognitive = 0.52, 95% CI [0.38, 0.67]; ps < .001), yielding significant indirect effects (neural: ab = -0.21, 95% CI [-0.31, -0.11], p = .001; cognitive: ab = 0.23, 95% CI [0.13, 0.33], p < .001). Faster processing speed at both neural and cognitive levels was associated with higher complex span performance, which in turn predicted higher cognitive ability, consistent with a 3 All confidence intervals reported in-text are delta-method based. All indirect effects reported in this section were additionally evaluated using bias-corrected bootstrap confidence intervals (5,000 resamples). Bootstrap CIs confirmed all indirect effects reported as significant via the delta method and additionally excluded zero for the binding-specific indirect effects in the parallel mediator models (see Supplementary Table S2).

mediation account in which working memory partially accounts for the association between processing speed and g. Note. However, the direct paths from processing speed to g remained significant after accounting for complex span (βneural = -0.28, 95% CI [-0.44, -0.12], p = .003; βcognitive = 0.25, 95% CI [0.08, 0.43], p = .007), and likelihood ratio tests confirmed that constraining these direct paths to zero significantly worsened model fit (neural: Δχ²(1) = 10.80, p = .001; cognitive: Δχ²(1) = 8.03, p = .005). Complex span performance thus only partially mediated the relationship between processing speed and g. The indirect effects accounted for a substantial share of the total effect (43% and 47%, respectively), but the remaining direct effects indicate that a substantial portion of the speed-g association is not captured by the executive working memory demands measured by complex span tasks.

Does Relational Binding Account for the Processing Speed-g Relationship?

We then tested whether binding performance mediated the speed-g relationship. Model fit was acceptable for both the neural processing speed model, χ²(146) = 254.86, p < .001, CFI = 0.90, RMSEA = 0.06, 90% CI [0.05, 0.07], and the cognitive processing speed model, χ²(61) = 155.26, p < .001, CFI = 0.89, RMSEA = 0.08, 90% CI [0.07, 0.10], though the CFI of the cognitive processing speed model fell slightly below the conventional threshold. We did not modify the model as the degree of misfit is still relatively low and the measurement model specification is identical to that of the complex span model, ensuring that the comparison between mediators is not confounded by differential fit. Standardized path estimates for both models are shown in Figure 4.

In both models, processing speed significantly predicted binding performance (βneural = -0.39, 95% CI [-0.61, -0.17], p = .005; βcognitive = 0.56, 95% CI [0.35, 0.78], p < .001), and binding performance significantly predicted g (βneural = 0.76, 95% CI [0.54, 0.98], p < .001; βcognitive = 0.83, 95% CI [0.53, 1.14], p = .004), yielding significant indirect effects (neural: ab = -0.29, 95% CI [-0.50, -0.09], p = .014; cognitive: ab = 0.47, 95% CI [0.16, 0.78], p = .008). Faster processing speed was associated with better binding performance, which in turn predicted higher cognitive ability, consistent with a mediation BINDING MEDIATES THE SPEED → G RELATIONSHIP 30 account in which the ability to form and maintain temporary bindings accounts for the effect of processing speed on g. Note. Panel A shows the model with neural processing speed (ERP peak latencies) as the predictor. Panel B shows the model with cognitive processing speed (drift rates) as the predictor. Circles represent latent variables; rectangles represent observed indicators. Bold values on structural paths are standardized coefficients (β) with 95% confidence intervals in brackets. Gray values indicate non-significant path coefficients. Small values adjacent to indicator arrows are standardized factor loadings. Dashed arrows denote factor loadings fixed to one.
