Bias-corrected bootstrap confidence intervals (5,000 resamples) confirmed all significant indirect effects. Fit indices are reported below each panel. N = 215.

Critically, the direct paths from processing speed to g were substantially reduced when binding was included as a mediator (βneural = -0.22, 95% CI [-0.44, 0.00], p = .064; βcognitive = 0.04, 95% CI [-0.28, 0.36], p = .828). Likelihood ratio tests indicated that constraining these direct paths to zero did not significantly worsen model fit (neural: Δχ²(1) = 2.73, p = .098; cognitive: Δχ²(1) = 0.04, p = .834). Because non-significant likelihood ratio tests constitute absence of evidence against full mediation rather than positive evidence for it, we additionally computed Akaike weights to quantify the relative support for the partial versus full mediation model. For the cognitive processing speed model, the Akaike weight favored the full mediation model (wfull = .73 vs. wpartial = .27), providing positive evidence that the speed-g relationship was fully accounted for by binding. For the neural processing speed model, the evidence was more equivocal (wpartial = .59 vs. wfull = .41 in favor of the partial mediation model), consistent with the borderline direct path in that model. Across both models, however, binding performance mediated the speed-g relationship more completely than complex span performance (with binding compared to complex span performance accounting for 57% vs. 43% and 93% vs. 47% of the total effect, respectively), with the direct path reduced from significant to non-significant in both speed operationalizations.

Competitive Mediation: Parallel Mediators

The preceding analyses established that complex span partially mediated the speed-g relationship, whereas binding mediated it more completely. However, because complex span tasks also require participants to maintain bindings between memoranda and serial positions, the two constructs share substantial variance. To test whether complex span and binding contributed unique mediation above and beyond the other, we estimated parallel mediator models in which both were entered simultaneously. Model fit was acceptable for both the neural processing speed model, χ²(200) = 364.26, p < .001, CFI = 0.88, RMSEA = 0.06, 90% CI [0.05, 0.07], and the cognitive processing speed model, χ²(97) = 223.41, p < .001, CFI = 0.89, RMSEA = 0.08, 90% CI [0.06, 0.09]. Model fit was somewhat reduced relative to the single-mediator models, as expected given the additional latent factor and the attendant increase in model complexity that further depresses the CFI. The RMSEA remained within acceptable bounds across both models.

When both mediators competed for the prediction of g, binding emerged as the stronger predictor in both models (βneural = 0.80, 95% CI [0.22, 1.38], p = .036; βcognitive = 0.79, 95% CI [0.05, 1.52], p = .082), whereas the unique contribution of complex span was effectively zero (βneural = -0.06, 95% CI [-0.60, 0.49], p = .837, βcognitive = 0.02, 95% CI [-0.56, 0.61], p = .939). The pattern of indirect effects mirrored this asymmetry: the indirect effect through binding was substantial in both models (neural: ab = -0.32, 95% CI [-0.64, -0.01], p = .060; cognitive: ab = 0.46, 95% CI [-0.05, 0.97], p = .086), whereas the indirect effect through complex span was negligible (neural: ab = 0.02, 95% CI [-0.20, 0.25], p = .838; cognitive: ab = 0.01, 95% CI [-0.25, 0.27], p = .939). However, the high collinearity between the two factors limited statistical power, and in the delta-method tests neither the individual binding indirect effects nor the difference between the two indirect pathways reached significance (neural: Δab = -0.35, 95% CI [-0.86, 0.17], p = .198; cognitive: Δab = 0.45, 95% CI [-0.30, 1.20], p = .241). Bias-corrected bootstrap confidence intervals, which accommodate the skewed sampling distribution of indirect effects, excluded zero for the binding-specific indirect effects in both models (neural: unstandardized ab = -0.69, 95% CI [-3.18, -0.17]; cognitive: unstandardized ab = 0.72, 95% CI [0.21, 5.00]), but not for the complex spanspecific indirect effects or for the difference between the two pathways (Supplementary Table S2).

Because the high collinearity between the complex span and binding factors (r = .79) inflated standard errors in the frequentist parallel mediator models, rendering neither individual indirect effect significant in the delta-method tests despite substantial point estimate differences, we additionally estimated Bayesian structural equation models. Unlike frequentist confidence intervals, which can only reject or fail to reject the null, Bayesian posterior distributions allow direct computation of the probability that one indirect effect exceeds the otherprecisely the comparison that the collinearity problem prevents the frequentist models from resolving. Bayesian models with weakly informative priors corroborated the overall pattern. Details of the Bayesian robustness analyses are provided in the Supplementary Materials (see section "Bayesian Robustness Analysis" and Supplementary Tables S3-S5). Note. Panel A shows posterior means and 95% credible intervals for competing indirect effects. ab = indirect effect; Δab = difference between binding and complex span indirect effects. The dashed vertical line marks zero. Estimates are on the unstandardized metric. Panel B shows posterior density distributions for competing indirect effects. Shaded regions show the full posterior of the indirect effect through binding (dark blue) and complex span (light blue). The dashed vertical line marks zero. For both neural and cognitive processing speed, the binding posterior is shifted away from zero while the complex span posterior straddles it.

In the Bayesian parallel mediator models (see Figure 5 and Supplementary Table S5), the indirect effect through binding had a 95% credible interval excluding zero for both neural (CrI [-2.79, -0.29]) and cognitive processing speed (CrI [0.23, 2.57]), whereas the indirect effect through complex span did not (neural: CrI [-0.39, 1.17]; cognitive: CrI [-0.72, 0.39]).

The posterior probability that the binding indirect effect exceeded the complex span indirect effect was .98 for neural and .96 for cognitive processing speed (Δab CrI excluded zero for neural but not cognitive processing speed). Taken together, the parallel mediator analyses consistently identified binding as the stronger pathway linking processing speed to general cognitive ability, with complex span contributing no unique variance beyond what it shares with binding, although the precision of the individual indirect effect estimates was limited by the substantial overlap between the two constructs.

Alternative Structural Arrangements

The converse ordering, where speed mediates the relationship between working memory capacity and g, is also defensible: if the focus of attention can be expanded to encompass more items, greater capacity may itself produce faster processing rather than resulting from it (Lemaire et al., 2018). Because the structural portion of the competitive model is saturated with respect to the latent covariance matrix (i.e., four constructs generate ten unique covariance elements, and the model estimates ten structural parameters), its fit cannot be taken as evidence for the ordering it imposes. Hence, we cannot use simple model comparisons to test whether a reverse ordering of constructs (e.g., introducing speed as a mediator of the association between working memory capacity and g) provides a better account of the data than the theoretically proposed model. We confirmed this by refitting the model in the fully reversed direction and as a simultaneous regression of g on all three constructs; all three parameterizations returned identical fit. As expected from their Markov equivalence, the single-mediator chain and its full reversal also had identical fit.

Nevertheless, we can test a restrictive implication of any proposed ordering using partial correlations. In a three-variable chain, the intermediate construct screens off the two outer ones. If A → B → C, then A and C are conditionally independent given B. Reversing the entire chain leaves that restriction unchanged, which is why direction is not identified.

However, moving a different construct into the intermediate position changes which partial correlation is required to vanish. We use the term statistical separator rather than mediator for the construct in this position to signal that the restriction concerns conditional independence rather than causal transmission. The three candidate separators therefore imply three mutually exclusive restrictionsr(speed, g | WMC) = 0, r(WMC, g | speed) = 0, and r(speed, WMC | g) = 0each nested within the saturated structural model but not within one another, and each costing exactly one degree of freedom.

First, to examine a restrictive implication of the reverse-direction account, we tested models in which processing speed was the sole separator between working memory and g. This restriction was rejected in all four construct sets, Δχ²(1) = 33.72 to 38.46, all p < .001, Akaike weights < .01. This result does not rule out an effect of working memory capacity on processing speed in a less restricted model; it rejects the stronger assumption that speed completely accounts for the association between working memory capacity and g.

The restriction implied by treating working memory as the separator is the test of the direct speed-g path already reported above; the values are repeated here so that the three orderings can be compared on a common footing. Treating binding as the separator imposed a restriction that was not rejected for either neural processing speed, Δχ²(1) = 2.73, p = .098, or cognitive processing speed, Δχ²(1) = 0.04, p = .834. The corresponding restriction was rejected for complex span, both for neural processing speed, Δχ²(1) = 10.80, p = .001, and cognitive processing speed, Δχ²(1) = 8.03, p = .005. Models treating g as the separator were also not rejected, Δχ²(1) = 0.21 to 3.22, p = .073 to .643. The g-separator model is equivalent to a common-cause parameterization in which g predicts both speed and working memory. Relative support for binding versus g as a statistical separator differed across the two speed operationalizations. For neural speed, the g-separator model was favored (Akaike weights of .78 for g vs. .22 for binding), whereas for cognitive speed the binding-separator model was modestly favored (.69 for binding vs. .31 for g). The analyses therefore did not consistently favor either conditional-independence structure. By contrast, the g-separator model was strongly favored over the complex span separator in both models (Akaike weights of .99 and .92 for g), indicating that binding is competitive with g as a separator whereas complex span is not. Importantly, these results distinguish binding from complex span but do not establish a causal ordering among binding, speed, and g.

Simultaneous Prediction of g

As a complementary description of the saturated four-construct model, we entered processing speed, complex span, and binding as simultaneous predictors of g. These models are reparameterizations of the theoretically proposed parallel mediator models above and therefore yield identical model fit. Binding retained a large conditional association with g in both models (βneural = 0.80, 95% CI [0.22, 1.38], p = .036, βcognitive = 0.79, 95% CI [0.05, 1.52], p = .082). Although the binding coefficient reached significance only in the neural model, dropping the binding path significantly degraded fit in both models, Δχ²(1) = 13.85, p < .001, and Δχ²(1) = 9.37, p = .002. The conditional coefficients for complex span were close to zero (βneural = -0.06, 95% CI [-0.60, 0.49], p = .837; βcognitive = 0.02, 95% CI [-0.56, 0.61], p = .939) and dropping this path did not degrade fit, Δχ²(1) ≤ 0.05, p ≥ .827. Processing speed showed no significant conditional association with g (βneural = -0.21, 95% CI [-0.42, -0.01], p = .059, βcognitive = 0.02, 95% CI [-0.30, 0.34], p = .885), and the corresponding drop-one tests did not reject either path restriction, Δχ²(1) ≤ 2.14, p ≥ .143. Together, the three predictors explained 73.5% (model with neural processing speed) and 67.1% (model with cognitive processing speed) of the variance in g. A supplementary commonality analysis yielded the same general pattern, with the largest unique component associated with binding and negligible unique variance associated with complex span.

Discussion

Across both neural and cognitive operationalizations of processing speed, individual differences in working memory binding accounted for the relationship between processing speed and general cognitive ability, fully for cognitive speed and substantially for neural speed. This finding converges with developmental evidence that increases in processing speed precede gains in working memory, which in turn precede gains in fluid ability (Fry & Hale, 1996 , 2000; Kail, 2007; Kail et al., 2016; Nettelbeck & Burns, 2010), and extends that work by identifying relational binding rather than the executive attention demands captured by complex span tasks as the specific component process that statistically accounts for the link.

Binding, Not Executive Attention, Carries the Link Between Processing Speed and

General Cognitive Ability

Critically, when both complex span and binding task performance were considered as parallel mediators, binding emerged as the dominant predictor of general cognitive ability across models of both neural and cognitive processing speed, with complex span contributing effectively zero unique variance. Bayesian parallel mediator models corroborated this pattern, with posterior probabilities of .98 (neural speed) and .96 (cognitive speed) that the indirect effect through binding exceeded that through complex span. This finding identifies binding as the working memory component that statistically accounts for the association between processing speed and general cognitive ability. Moreover, it suggests that complex span mediates the speed-g association only to the degree to which performance in typical complex span tasks also reflects bindingthat is, the formation and maintenance of associations between memoranda and serial positions across the retention and secondary task intervalsas reflected in the high correlation between the two factors (r = .79). We note that binding is not the only process shared by complex span and binding tasks, as both also require stimulus encoding, maintenance through attentional refreshing, and cue-based retrieval and the present data cannot determine which shared process drives the high correlation between the two factors.

That binding tasks predict cognitive ability at least as well as complex span tasks is well established (Colom et al., 2008; Oberauer et al., 2008; Unsworth & Engle, 2007; Wilhelm et al., 2013). What the present results add is that this holds specifically for the variance shared with processing speed. Specifically, the critical observation is that the variance unique to complex span (plausibly reflecting dual-task coordination and interference management from the secondary task) does not contribute to the prediction of g beyond what is shared with binding tasks. Whatever the shared process is, it is captured at least as well by the structurally simpler binding tasks as by complex span tasks, which argues against the executive attention account's claim that the attentionally demanding aspects of working memory are what make it central to cognition. The claim here concerns the speed-g pathway specifically; whether the executive demands of complex span contribute to cognitive ability through routes unrelated to processing speed is not addressed by these data. This interpretation is consistent with the observation that complex span only partially accounted for the speed-g association when entered as the sole mediator: because it captures both binding and executive attention demands, it provides a less focused index of the specific process that accounts for the link. These findings do not imply that the executive demands of complex span tasks are irrelevant to task performance. Dual-task coordination, interference management, and the maintenance of goal-relevant information against distraction are genuine processing demands that contribute to the difficulty of complex span tasks at the within-person level. However, the present results suggest that these demands do not constitute a separable source of individual differences that independently covaries with both processing speed and general cognitive ability. This interpretation converges with evidence that attention control is psychometrically inseparable from processing speed at the level of individual differences (Löffler et al., 2024; Rey-Mermet et al., 2025). If executive attention and processing speed share the same individual-differences variance, as both studies suggest, then executive attention cannot serve as a separable mediator between speed and g, because there is no unique executive attention variance left to carry an indirect effect once processing speed is already in the model. The present data are consistent with this account, though they bear on it indirectly. What they show directly is that the g-relevant variance in complex span is not exhausted by processing speedcomplex span retained a substantial partial association with g when speed was controlledbut is almost entirely shared with binding, where the corresponding partial association was approximately zero. The executive demands unique to complex span are therefore not what connects it to g, whether or not those demands are themselves absorbed by processing speed. This stands in contrast to Mashburn et al. (2024) who argue that executive attention is the more fundamental mechanism and that speed tasks predict intelligence only insofar as they index attentional efficiency. The present findings, together with Löffler et al. (2024), suggest the opposite directionality: what appears as attention control in executive function tasks may reflect speed of information uptake rather than a separable control capacity. The binding account offers a potential resolution to this debate by identifying a more specific shared process: the efficiency with which temporary bindings are formed and maintained is inherently speed-dependent, requires the attentional focusing that executive function tasks demand, and constitutes the core working memory operation supporting complex reasoning. On this view, speed, executive function, and binding do not reflect three independent constructs competing for explanatory priority, but partially overlapping windows onto the same underlying computational efficiency.

Why Might Faster Processing Be Associated With Better Binding?

If binding is the critical component through which the speed-g association is reproduced, a natural question is why faster processing should facilitate binding in particular. Current computational models of working memory binding do not include processing speed as a parameter, treating binding precision as fixed across individuals, leaving a theoretical gap (Oberauer & Lin, 2017 , 2024). We propose, as a framework for future empirical test rather than a conclusion supported by the present data, that processing speed may affect binding quality through two complementary pathways.

The first pathway operates through encoding resource dynamics. Popov and Reder (2020) formalized a limited encoding resource that depletes with each item encoded and recovers over time, with subsequent work demonstrating that this resource affects the probability of forming a binding rather than the precision of successfully formed bindings (Popov et al., 2022). Converging evidence that free time between items produces a global, proactive memory benefit is consistent with time-dependent resource recovery rather than maintenance or rehearsal (Mızrak & Oberauer, 2021). Individual differences in processing speed may modulate this pathway by enabling adequate encoding with less resource expenditure per item and by allowing more complete short-term consolidation of each binding before the next item arrives (Jolicoeur & Dell'Acqua, 1998; Ricker & Hardman, 2017), reducing binding failures at longer set sizes.

The second pathway operates through the global signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) of cognitive processing (Oberauer et al., 2026). In the diffusion model, drift rate is the ratio of evidence accumulation rate to within-trial noise. In interference-based models of working memory, an analogous ratio governs retrieval accuracy: the activation of the target binding relative to competing bindings and background noise (Oberauer & Lin, 2017 , 2024). If a common neural efficiency parameter governs both, individuals with higher drift rates should also form bindings with greater precision on both the content dimension (yielding narrower feature representations) and the context dimension (yielding more selective cue-based retrieval). Higher context precision is especially consequential, as it determines how much interference from non-target bindings contaminates retrieval, and even modest improvements can substantially reduce set-size effects, because the influence of each non-target binding declines exponentially with context distance.

These two pathways are complementary rather than competing: the first governs whether bindings are formed at all, the second governs the fidelity of successfully formed bindings. Together, they predict that individuals with faster processing speed maintain more numerous and more precise bindings, which recent evidence suggests is the aspect of relational processing most closely tied to reasoning ability (Chuderski, 2019; Jastrzębski et al., 2020).

The present data establish that binding statistically accounts for the speed-g association but cannot isolate which aspect of binding is most speed-dependent. Future work using computational modeling approachessuch as the Interference Measurement Model (Oberauer et al., 2017) or the Memory Measurement Model (Oberauer & Lewandowsky, 2019)could decompose binding performance into encoding precision, interference susceptibility, and retrieval fidelity, and test which component is most strongly predicted by individual differences in drift rates and ERP latencies.

Asymmetry Between Neural and Cognitive Processing Speed

In the present study, binding fully mediated the relationship between cognitive processing speed and general cognitive ability, whereas the results were more equivocal for the relationship between neural processing speed and general cognitive ability. While the direct paths from both neural and cognitive processing speed to g were no longer significant after introducing binding as a mediator, model comparisons yielded definite results only for cognitive processing speed and were more inconclusive for neural processing speed. Several factors may contribute to this asymmetry. First, cognitive processing speed, binding, and general cognitive ability were all measured behaviorally, whereas neural processing speed introduces a different measurement level (electrophysiology). Hence, shared measurement variance may contribute to the somewhat clearer mediation path on the behavioral than on the neural level. However, the behavioral measures do not share task-specific variance (drift rates were derived from elementary cognitive tasks, binding scores from working memory tasks, and g from a broad intelligence battery), limiting the scope of common method variance even within the behavioral measurement level. Second, ERP peak latencies are known to be notoriously noisy estimates, resulting in more measurement error at the indicator level compared to drift rates, which may propagate into less precisely estimated latent variables.

Consistent with this, our model-based composite reliability estimates suggest lower factor reliabilities at the latent variable level for neural than cognitive measures of processing speed (see Table 1). Third, and most theoretically informative, drift rates and ERP latencies, while correlated, do not capture identical variance (Schubert et al., 2019 (Schubert et al., , 2020)). Drift rates reflect the efficiency of evidence accumulation across the full decision process, while ERP latencies index discrete stages of neural processing. Some neural speed variance may therefore influence cognitive ability through pathways other than bindinga possibility consistent with evidence that later ERP components such as the P3 index attentional and working memory updating processes that may contribute to cognitive ability independently of binding efficiency (Polich, 2007). These distinct pathways could sustain a residual direct effect from neural speed to g that is not observed for cognitive speed, where drift rates may more completely overlap with the variance relevant to binding. Taken together, these considerations suggest that the asymmetry likely reflects a combination of methodological and substantive factors, but the overall pattern remains convergent: across both operationalizations, binding emerged as the primary pathway linking processing speed to general cognitive ability.

Limitations

Several limitations warrant consideration. First, the cross-sectional design precludes causal inference. The mediation structure is theoretically motivated by the developmental cascade literature, including longitudinal evidence that changes in processing speed precede changes in working memory over one-year intervals (Kail, 2007) and across the lifespan (Nettelbeck & Burns, 2010). However, the present data cannot rule out alternative causal architectures. A common-cause model, in which some biological property or metacognitive adjustments drive both processing speed and binding capacity in parallel, remains viable and would produce the same cross-sectional mediation pattern. Longitudinal designs tracking within-person change, or experimental manipulations that selectively alter processing speed and assess downstream effects on binding, are needed to adjudicate between these accounts.

Second, the binding tasks employed here, while simpler than complex span tasks, are not process-pure measures of binding per se. Performance on letter-color, word-number, and location-letter binding tasks also requires stimulus encoding, maintenance against interference, retrieval, and other processes that extend beyond the formation of temporary associations. Moreover, the nature of binding differs between the two task types. In complex span tasks, participants bind individual memoranda (up to 7 letters or grid positions) to serial positions within a sequence. In the binding tasks used here, participants encode pairs of arbitrarily associated features (up to 6 pairs per trial), requiring the concurrent representation of up to 12 individual elements even though only one element per pair is probed at retrieval.

The binding tasks may therefore place greater demands on encoding and short-term maintenance than complex span tasks, despite their simpler task structure. This asymmetry means that the two task types do not isolate identical binding operations, and the superior mediation performance of binding tasks could partly reflect their higher memory load at encoding rather than a purer measure of the binding process itself. The present data therefore cannot isolate which specific sub-process of binding is most speed-dependent. Neurocognitive psychometric approaches, using computational models such as the Interference Measurement Model (Oberauer et al., 2017) or the Memory Measurement Model (Oberauer & Lewandowsky, 2019), could decompose binding task performance into different components and test which is most strongly predicted by processing speed (Göttmann et al., 2025).

Third, the parallel mediator models suffered from reduced statistical power due to the high collinearity between the complex span and binding factors (r = .79). This collinearity resulted in wide confidence intervals for the individual indirect effects in the frequentist models, with both binding-specific indirect effects reaching only marginal significance in the delta-method tests (p = .060 and .086 for neural and cognitive speed, respectively), although their bias-corrected bootstrap confidence intervals exclude zero. The Bayesian analyses provided more decisive evidence, with posterior probabilities of at least .96 that the binding indirect effect was larger than the complex span indirect effect. Nonetheless, replication in larger samples would strengthen confidence in the parallel mediation results specifically, even though the single-mediator findings are robust.

Future Directions

Beyond addressing these limitations, the present findings open several productive lines of inquiry. Most pressing is the need to establish the causal direction of the relationship between speed and binding. Experimental designs that selectively manipulate processing speed (e.g., by introducing perceptual noise or degraded stimulus quality) and assess downstream effects on binding task performance would provide a direct test of whether slower processing causally impairs binding, as the mediation structure implies. Conversely, manipulations that selectively tax binding (e.g., by increasing the number of to-be-bound features or the similarity among bindings) while holding encoding demands constant could clarify whether binding capacity constrains processing speed in the reverse direction.

A second priority is to determine which sub-process of bindingencoding precision, cue-binding fidelity, or retrievalis most speed-dependent. Continuous-report paradigms, in which participants reproduce a bound feature on a continuous scale, can be modeled with mixture models (Bays et al., 2009; Zhang & Luck, 2008) or with the Interference Measurement Model (Oberauer et al., 2017), both of which decompose response distributions into theoretically interpretable parameters. Of particular diagnostic value is the distinction between precision (the fidelity of a stored representation given successful retrieval), misbinding rate (the probability of retrieving a feature bound to the wrong item), and, in the interference model, cue-binding precision (the sharpness of the generalization gradient along the retrieval-cue dimension). Hierarchical Bayesian estimation frameworks for these types of models yield reliable person-level parameter estimates with feasible trial counts, making them suitable for the kind of individual-differences design needed here (Göttmann et al., 2025; Oberauer et al., 2017). The critical test would be whether processing speed predicts misbinding rate or cue-binding precision, which would indicate that speed protects the binding operation itself, or precision, which would indicate that speed enhances the fidelity of encoded representations more broadly.

Third, extending the present framework to developmental and aging samples would test whether binding carries the speed-g mediation across the full lifespan, including contexts in which the speed-WMC-g cascade has been most extensively documented but where the specific role of binding has not been isolated.

Conclusion

The century-old observation that faster thinkers tend to be smarter thinkers has resisted mechanistic explanation. The present findings offer a specific account: the association between processing speed and general cognitive ability is statistically accounted for by the ability to form and maintain temporary bindings in working memory. When this binding component is accounted for, processing speed, whether measured at the neural or cognitive level, no longer independently predicts general cognitive ability. It is not the executive demands of complex span tasks, nor a diffuse attention control capacity, that carries this link, but the more elemental operation of associating disparate pieces of information into coherent bindings. Binding, in this view, is the cognitive intermediary that links processing efficiency to complex reasoning.

Constraints on Generality

We expect the core finding that working memory binding mediates the speed-g relationship to generalize broadly, but several sample characteristics constrain this expectation. Participants were recruited from a community sample in a German university city and were aged 18-40. Although the sample exhibited substantial variance in cognitive ability scores (BIS: mean IQ of MBIS = 96.82, SDBIS = 11.09foot_1 ; APM: mean IQ of MAPM = 102.23foot_2 , SDAPM = 18.28), it was age-restricted by design and drawn from a population with high average educational attainment. The age restriction is particularly relevant because the speed → WMC → g cascade has been most extensively studied in developmental and aging samples (Fry & Hale, 1996; Kail, 2007; Kail et al., 2016; Nettelbeck & Burns, 2010; Salthouse, 1996), where the range of variation in processing speed is considerably wider than in young and middle-aged adults. Whether binding specifically rather than working memory capacity more broadly carries the mediation in these age groups remains an open empirical question, as agerelated slowing may affect multiple working memory sub-processes simultaneously.

Furthermore, binding has been shown to be particularly sensitive to age differences (Naveh-Benjamin, 2000; Oberauer & Lewandowsky, 2019), raising the possibility that binding may play an even more prominent mediating role in aging samples than observed here. We also note that participants were not selected on the basis of clinical or neurological status; generalizability to populations with working memory impairments (e.g., individuals with ADHD, traumatic brain injury, or neurodegenerative conditions) should not be assumed.

Finally, all tasks were administered in German to a predominantly German-speaking sample; while the constructs under investigation are not language-dependent in any obvious way, cross-cultural replication would strengthen confidence in the generality of the findings.

Note. B = unstandardized estimate; Boot SE = bootstrap standard error; 95% BC CI = bias-corrected 95% bootstrap confidence interval; β = standardized estimate. CS = complex span; Bind = binding. Path labels: a = speed → mediator; b = mediator → g; c′ = direct effect (speed → g, controlling for mediator); ab = indirect effect; c = total effect; Δab = difference between indirect effects (binding minus complex span). Bootstrap p-values are Wald tests based on the bootstrap standard error (z = B / Boot SE), whereas confidence intervals use the bias-corrected (BC) percentile method, which adjusts for median bias in the bootstrap distribution (Efron, 1987). Because indirect effects have inherently skewed sampling distributions, BC confidence intervals provide more accurate coverage than the percentile method (MacKinnon et al., 2004). Where p-values and confidence intervals disagree, inference should be based on whether the BC interval excludes zero, consistent with current methodological recommendations for indirect effect testing (Preacher & Hayes, 2008). The lower convergence rates in the parallel mediator models (95.0% and 92.4%) are likely due to the high collinearity between the complex span and binding factors (r = .79). All models were estimated with maximum likelihood and full information maximum likelihood for missing data. N = 215.

BINDING MEDIATES THE SPEED → G RELATIONSHIP (SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIAL) 4

Bayesian Robustness Analysis

Method

All mediation models reported in the main text were re-estimated using Bayesian structural equation modeling (BSEM) via the blavaan package (version 0.6-1; Merkle et al., 2021), which interfaces with Stan for Hamiltonian Monte Carlo sampling. Model specifications (measurement models, structural paths, defined indirect effect parameters) were identical to the frequentist lavaan models.

Priors. Weakly informative N(0, 3) priors were placed on all regression coefficients. All observed variables were z-standardized before analysis. With a standard deviation of 3, these priors are wide relative to the largest estimated coefficients (posterior means up to ≈ 2.5) while providing sufficient regularization to prevent posterior funnels in smaller measurement models.

Factor loading priors were N(0, 10), and residual and factor standard deviations received Gamma(1, 0.5) priors, following blavaan defaults.

Sampling.

Each model was estimated with four MCMC chains of 2,000 warmup and 4,000 post-warmup iterations (16,000 total posterior draws). Drift rate single-mediator models used adapt_delta = 0.95 and max_treedepth = 12 to suppress divergent transitions. All chains were initialized at the maximum-likelihood estimates of the respective model, and all models used a fixed random seed.

Convergence. Convergence was assessed via split-chain 𝑹 ̂ (Merkle et al., 2021) (all < 1.01; Vehtari et al., 2021) and bulk effective sample size (all > 2,300). In addition, the four chains of each model reached the same mean log posterior density (maximum difference: 0.7), and no divergent transitions occurred. All models converged successfully (see Table S3). In preliminary runs with blavaan's default starting values, a single chain of some cognitive BINDING MEDIATES THE SPEED → G RELATIONSHIP (SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIAL) 5 processing speed models became trapped in a spurious mode with a much lower posterior density (mean log posterior ≈ 33 units below the other chains). These runs were discarded; all reported models were re-estimated with chains initialized at the maximum-likelihood estimates.

Single-Mediator Models.

All four indirect effects were corroborated (see Table S4): standardized indirect effects agreed with the ML estimates within Δβ < .06 (all paths: Δβ < .08), and all indirect-effect CrIs excluded zero.

Slight differences from ML estimates are consistent with posterior regularization under weakly informative priors, particularly for variance parameters.

Parallel Mediator Models. In both models, the indirect effect through binding had a 95% CrI excluding zero while the indirect effect through complex span did not (see Table S5). The posterior probability that the binding indirect effect exceeded the complex span indirect effect was P(|abBind| > |abCS|) = .98 for neural processing speed and P(abBind > abCS) = .96 for cognitive processing speed.

Table 2

Latent Correlations Estimated From a Joint Measurement Model Containing All Five Latent

Because standardized CIs are based on delta-method standard errors of the standardized estimates, they can occasionally exclude zero when the test of the corresponding unstandardized coefficient is not significant; inference is based on the unstandardized tests and, for nested models, on likelihood ratio tests.

Note that the IQ score of the BIS is normed to a standard deviation of 10.

Note that the average IQ score of the APM may be slightly overestimated because the norm data are based on an administration with a 40-minute time limit.
